What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that my eating has gotten so off that some days I barely get down coffee and a granola bar before a shift, and my scrubs have been hanging loose even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. Smith. Well, I guess what brings me in is that my eating has just gotten so off lately. Like, some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before I clock in for my shift. I work as a healthcare support worker, and honestly, the coffee in our breakroom has been terrible ever since administration switched suppliers last month. It’s this cheap dark roast that just tastes like burnt dirt. I don't know who makes these purchasing decisions, probably someone in corporate who never even steps foot on our floor... but, um, anyway, yeah, I'm barely eating. 

And I noticed my scrubs have been hanging really loose on me lately, even though I'm not trying to lose weight at all. Which is annoying because I just bought these new ones with the extra cargo pockets on the thigh—you really need those pockets for all the tape and extra supplies when you're doing patient transport—and now they just sag right off my waist. The drawstring is pulled as tight as it goes.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.

I tried spraying WD-40 on one of the chairs once, but my supervisor caught me and said it wasn't protocol. Protocol! Like we have a protocol for squeaky wheels in a damp, creepy basement. But yeah, no, I've never felt this down before. This is all new.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the changes in your appetite and feeling down, have you noticed that you've lost interest or pleasure in doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. I used to love going for runs in Van Cortlandt Park, you know? Taking my camera out, getting some good shots of the trees, maybe meeting up with a few buddies to play chess. But now... I just tell myself there's no point. I end up just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or staring at my budget spreadsheet. And speaking of the budget, the price of groceries has just gotten insane. I was at the store the other day just to get coffee, right? Because like I said, that's all I'm really having. And a regular bag of the store-brand coffee was almost nine dollars. Nine dollars! I remember when you could get a premium roast for that much. And don't even get me started on eggs. I don't eat them right now, but I saw the price tag and nearly had a heart attack. The cashier—I think her name tag said Brenda—she was telling me that people have been complaining all week. She looked exhausted, honestly. Being on your feet all day dealing with angry customers... it's a lot like being at the hospital, really. People take out their frustrations on the person right in front of them, even if it's not their fault. Like this one patient we had in room 412 last month... he threw his remote at the TV because the sports channel was blacked out. But anyway... yeah, I just don't have the energy or care to do the things I used to like. Just sit at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or do you feel so restless that you find it hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I haven't... I haven't made any kind of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But yeah, sometimes I do catch myself just wishing I wouldn't wake up. Like, it would just be so much easier if I could disappear, you know? Just poof, gone. No more dragging myself out of bed, no more dealing with the squeaky transport chairs. It reminds me of this magic show my uncle took me to when I was a kid. This guy, he called himself The Great Zamboni or something, he had this huge cabinet on stage. He put his assistant in there, closed the doors, spun it around, and when he opened it—she was gone. Poof. I always wondered how they did that trick. I mean, I know there's a trap door or mirrors involved. My cousin Eddie tried to explain it to me once. Eddie was always trying to figure out how things worked. He took apart his dad's lawnmower when he was twelve and couldn't put it back together. His dad was furious. They had to pay a guy fifty bucks to come fix it, and fifty bucks was a lot of money back then. Eddie ended up becoming an engineer, though, so I guess taking the lawnmower apart paid off in the end. He lives out in Seattle now. Rains a lot out there. I don't think I could handle all that rain. But yeah, I don't want to kill myself, I just... I just think about how nice it would be to just not be here anymore. Just disappear like that assistant in the cabinet.

I'm not worried about gaining weight. I just barely eat my coffee and a granola bar, and that's it. I had to use a safety pin to attach it to my scrubs, which is just a hazard waiting to happen when you're helping patients out of bed. I put in a request for a new one with HR, but you know how they are. They said it takes three to five business days to process a replacement, but with the holiday weekend coming up, who knows when I'll actually get it. I told Gary about it—he's the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—and he said it's just steam pressure, but honestly, it sounds like a snake. A really big, angry snake. And the floors down there are always slightly damp, which makes the wheels on the transport chairs squeak. It drives me crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on one of the chairs once, but my supervisor caught me and said it wasn't protocol. Protocol! Like we have a protocol for squeaky wheels in a damp, creepy basement. But yeah, no, I've never felt this down before. I used to love going for runs in Van Cortlandt Park, you know? Taking my camera out, getting some good shots of the trees, maybe meeting up with a few buddies to play chess. But now... I just tell myself there's no point. I end up just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or staring at my budget spreadsheet. And speaking of the budget, the price of groceries has just gotten insane. I was at the store the other day just to get coffee, right? Because like I said, that's all I'm really having. And a regular bag of the store-brand coffee was almost nine dollars. Nine dollars! I remember when you could get a premium roast for that much. And don't even get me started on eggs. I don't eat them right now, but I saw the price tag and nearly had a heart attack. The cashier—I think her name tag said Brenda—she was telling me that people have been complaining all week. She looked exhausted, honestly. Being on your feet all day dealing with angry customers... it's a lot like being at the hospital, really. People take out their frustrations on the person right in front of them, even if it's not their fault. Like this one patient we had in room 412 last month... he threw his remote at the TV because the sports channel was blacked out. But anyway... yeah, I just don't have the energy or care to do the things I used to like. Just sit at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or do you feel so restless that you find it hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.\n\nDoctor: With all of this heaviness and the feeling that everything is pointless, have you found yourself having any thoughts that life is not worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't... I haven't made any kind of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But yeah, sometimes I do catch myself just wishing I wouldn't wake up. Like, it would just be so much easier if I could disappear, you know? Just poof, gone. No more dragging myself out of bed, no more dealing with the squeaky transport chairs. It reminds me of this magic show my uncle took me to when I was a kid. This guy, he called himself The Great Zamboni or something, he had this huge cabinet on stage. He put his assistant in there, closed the doors, spun it around, and when he opened it—she was gone. Poof. I always wondered how they did that trick. I mean, I know there's a trap door or mirrors involved. My cousin Eddie tried to explain it to me once. Eddie was always trying to figure out how things worked. He took apart his dad's lawnmower when he was twelve and couldn't put it back together. His dad was furious. They had to pay a guy fifty bucks to come fix it, and fifty bucks was a lot of money back then. Eddie ended up becoming an engineer, though, so I guess taking the lawnmower apart paid off in the end. He lives out in Seattle now. Rains a lot out there. I don't think I could handle all that rain. But yeah, I don't want to kill myself, I just... I just think about how nice it would be to just not be here anymore. Just disappear like that assistant in the cabinet.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you still able to perform your duties at work, or has your condition made it impossible for you to carry out your job responsibilities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 8\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's getting harder, honestly. I'm still showing up, but... I've started making these really avoidable mistakes. Like, I'll forget where I put the clean linens or I'll need the nurses to repeat simple requests, like two or three times. It's embarrassing. I just can't seem to focus. It's like my brain is full of cotton. Which is weird, because I used to be so sharp. I was the guy everyone came to when they needed something found quickly. I remember this one time, Dr. Henderson lost his favorite pen—this fancy silver one his wife gave him for their anniversary. He was tearing the nurses' station apart looking for it. I found it in about two minutes, rolled under the edge of the computer cart. He was so relieved, he bought me a coffee the next day. A large one, too, from that cafe down the street that roasts their own beans. Their coffee is amazing, but the line is always out the door. I don't know how people have the patience to stand in line for twenty minutes just for a cup of coffee. Especially in the winter when the wind is whipping down the avenue. It cuts right through your coat. But anyway... yeah, work is tough right now. I don't even go to chess nights with my buddies anymore, or take my camera out for walks. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game or even act normal around them. I feel like I'd just mess it up or say the wrong thing.

It’s this cheap dark roast that just tastes like burnt dirt. I don't know who makes these purchasing decisions, probably someone in corporate who never even steps foot on our floor... but, um, anyway, yeah, I'm barely eating. \n\nAnd I noticed my scrubs have been hanging really loose on me lately, even though I'm not trying to lose weight at all. Which is annoying because I just bought these new ones with the extra cargo pockets on the thigh—you really need those pockets for all the tape and extra supplies when you're doing patient transport—and now they just sag right off my waist. The drawstring is pulled as tight as it goes.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food or eating less because you are worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. Like I said, I'm definitely not trying to lose weight. I just... I don't know, I just don't have the appetite for it. I look at food and it just seems like too much effort to even chew it. Which is weird because I used to love stopping by this little diner near the hospital after a long shift. They make this incredible meatloaf—like, real homestyle meatloaf with the crispy edges and the thick brown gravy. The waitress there, Shirley, she always knows exactly how I like my potatoes, too. Mashed, but with the skins still mixed in. She's been working there for, gosh, must be fifteen years. It's... well, most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge. It's just this weight in my chest. And speaking of my badge, the clip on it broke last week, the retractable kind? I had to use a safety pin to attach it to my scrubs, which is just a hazard waiting to happen when you're helping patients out of bed. I put in a request for a new one with HR, but you know how they are. They said it takes three to five business days to process a replacement, but with the holiday weekend coming up, who knows when I'll actually get it. My friend Gary in maintenance said he waited two weeks for his. Two weeks! Just to get a piece of plastic with a string in it. But, um, yeah, I just feel really down and heavy most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way, or experienced a period of feeling this down and having such a low appetite, at any point in your life before now?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had bad days, sure, but nothing like this where I just... where I don't even want to eat and everything feels so heavy. I've always been a pretty steady guy. Like, even when they renovated the west wing of the hospital and we had to detour all the transport beds through the basement tunnels. Those tunnels are so creepy, by the way. The fluorescent lights flicker constantly, and there's this one pipe near the old laundry chute that makes a hissing sound every time you walk past it. I told Gary about it—he's the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—and he said it's just steam pressure, but honestly, it sounds like a snake. A really big, angry snake. And the floors down there are always slightly damp, which makes the wheels on the transport chairs squeak. It drives me crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on one of the chairs once, but my supervisor caught me and said it wasn't protocol. Protocol! Like we have a protocol for squeaky wheels in a damp, creepy basement. But yeah, no, I've never felt this down before. This is all new.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the changes in your appetite and feeling down, have you noticed that you've lost interest or pleasure in doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. And speaking of the budget, the price of groceries has just gotten insane. I was at the store the other day just to get coffee, right? Because like I said, that's all I'm really having. And a regular bag of the store-brand coffee was almost nine dollars. Nine dollars! I remember when you could get a premium roast for that much. And don't even get me started on eggs. I don't eat them right now, but I saw the price tag and nearly had a heart attack. The cashier—I think her name tag said Brenda—she was telling me that people have been complaining all week. She looked exhausted, honestly. Being on your feet all day dealing with angry customers... it's a lot like being at the hospital, really. People take out their frustrations on the person right in front of them, even if it's not their fault. Like this one patient we had in room 412 last month... he threw his remote at the TV because the sports channel was blacked out. But anyway... yeah, I just don't have the energy or care to do the things I used to like. Just sit at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or do you feel so restless that you find it hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.\n\nDoctor: With all of this heaviness and the feeling that everything is pointless, have you found yourself having any thoughts that life is not worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't... I haven't made any kind of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But yeah, sometimes I do catch myself just wishing I wouldn't wake up. Like, it would just be so much easier if I could disappear, you know? Just poof, gone. No more dragging myself out of bed, no more dealing with the squeaky transport chairs. It reminds me of this magic show my uncle took me to when I was a kid. This guy, he called himself The Great Zamboni or something, he had this huge cabinet on stage. He put his assistant in there, closed the doors, spun it around, and when he opened it—she was gone. Poof. I always wondered how they did that trick. I mean, I know there's a trap door or mirrors involved. My cousin Eddie tried to explain it to me once. Eddie was always trying to figure out how things worked. He took apart his dad's lawnmower when he was twelve and couldn't put it back together. His dad was furious. They had to pay a guy fifty bucks to come fix it, and fifty bucks was a lot of money back then. Eddie ended up becoming an engineer, though, so I guess taking the lawnmower apart paid off in the end. He lives out in Seattle now. Rains a lot out there. I don't think I could handle all that rain. But yeah, I don't want to kill myself, I just... I just think about how nice it would be to just not be here anymore. Just disappear like that assistant in the cabinet.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your duties at work, or has your condition made it impossible for you to carry out your job responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's getting harder, honestly. I'm still showing up, but... I've started making these really avoidable mistakes. Like, I'll forget where I put the clean linens or I'll need the nurses to repeat simple requests, like two or three times. It's embarrassing. I just can't seem to focus. It's like my brain is full of cotton. Which is weird, because I used to be so sharp. I was the guy everyone came to when they needed something found quickly. I remember this one time, Dr. Henderson lost his favorite pen—this fancy silver one his wife gave him for their anniversary. He was tearing the nurses' station apart looking for it. I found it in about two minutes, rolled under the edge of the computer cart. He was so relieved, he bought me a coffee the next day. A large one, too, from that cafe down the street that roasts their own beans. Their coffee is amazing, but the line is always out the door. I don't know how people have the patience to stand in line for twenty minutes just for a cup of coffee. Especially in the winter when the wind is whipping down the avenue. It cuts right through your coat. But anyway... yeah, work is tough right now. I don't even go to chess nights with my buddies anymore, or take my camera out for walks. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game or even act normal around them. I feel like I'd just mess it up or say the wrong thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well, I'm still working. I haven't taken a leave or anything, but... yeah, like I said, I'm making those dumb mistakes. And speaking of my badge, the clip on it broke last week, the retractable kind? I had to use a safety pin to attach it to my scrubs, which is just a hazard waiting to happen when you're helping patients out of bed. I put in a request for a new one with HR, but you know how they are. They said it takes three to five business days to process a replacement, but with the holiday weekend coming up, who knows when I'll actually get it. My friend Gary in maintenance said he waited two weeks for his. Two weeks! Just to get a piece of plastic with a string in it. But, um, yeah, I just feel really down and heavy most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way, or experienced a period of feeling this down and having such a low appetite, at any point in your life before now?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had bad days, sure, but nothing like this where I just... where I don't even want to eat and everything feels so heavy. I've always been a pretty steady guy. Like, even when they renovated the west wing of the hospital and we had to detour all the transport beds through the basement tunnels. Those tunnels are so creepy, by the way. The fluorescent lights flicker constantly, and there's this one pipe near the old laundry chute that makes a hissing sound every time you walk past it. I told Gary about it—he's the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—and he said it's just steam pressure, but honestly, it sounds like a snake. A really big, angry snake. And the floors down there are always slightly damp, which makes the wheels on the transport chairs squeak. It drives me crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on one of the chairs once, but my supervisor caught me and said it wasn't protocol. Protocol! Like we have a protocol for squeaky wheels in a damp, creepy basement. But yeah, no, I've never felt this down before. This is all new.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the changes in your appetite and feeling down, have you noticed that you've lost interest or pleasure in doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. I used to love going for runs in Van Cortlandt Park, you know? Taking my camera out, getting some good shots of the trees, maybe meeting up with a few buddies to play chess. But now... I just tell myself there's no point. I end up just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or staring at my budget spreadsheet. And speaking of the budget, the price of groceries has just gotten insane. I was at the store the other day just to get coffee, right? Because like I said, that's all I'm really having. And a regular bag of the store-brand coffee was almost nine dollars. Nine dollars! I remember when you could get a premium roast for that much. And don't even get me started on eggs. I don't eat them right now, but I saw the price tag and nearly had a heart attack. The cashier—I think her name tag said Brenda—she was telling me that people have been complaining all week. She looked exhausted, honestly. Being on your feet all day dealing with angry customers... it's a lot like being at the hospital, really. People take out their frustrations on the person right in front of them, even if it's not their fault. Like this one patient we had in room 412 last month... he threw his remote at the TV because the sports channel was blacked out. But anyway... yeah, I just don't have the energy or care to do the things I used to like. Just sit at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or do you feel so restless that you find it hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.\n\nDoctor: With all of this heaviness and the feeling that everything is pointless, have you found yourself having any thoughts that life is not worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't... I haven't made any kind of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But yeah, sometimes I do catch myself just wishing I wouldn't wake up. Like, it would just be so much easier if I could disappear, you know? Just poof, gone. No more dragging myself out of bed, no more dealing with the squeaky transport chairs. It reminds me of this magic show my uncle took me to when I was a kid. This guy, he called himself The Great Zamboni or something, he had this huge cabinet on stage. He put his assistant in there, closed the doors, spun it around, and when he opened it—she was gone. Poof. I always wondered how they did that trick. I mean, I know there's a trap door or mirrors involved. My cousin Eddie tried to explain it to me once. Eddie was always trying to figure out how things worked. He took apart his dad's lawnmower when he was twelve and couldn't put it back together. His dad was furious. They had to pay a guy fifty bucks to come fix it, and fifty bucks was a lot of money back then. Eddie ended up becoming an engineer, though, so I guess taking the lawnmower apart paid off in the end. He lives out in Seattle now. Rains a lot out there. I don't think I could handle all that rain. But yeah, I don't want to kill myself, I just... I just think about how nice it would be to just not be here anymore. Just disappear like that assistant in the cabinet.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your duties at work, or has your condition made it impossible for you to carry out your job responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's getting harder, honestly. I'm still showing up, but... I've started making these really avoidable mistakes. Like, I'll forget where I put the clean linens or I'll need the nurses to repeat simple requests, like two or three times. It's embarrassing. I just can't seem to focus. It's like my brain is full of cotton. Which is weird, because I used to be so sharp. I was the guy everyone came to when they needed something found quickly. I remember this one time, Dr. Henderson lost his favorite pen—this fancy silver one his wife gave him for their anniversary. He was tearing the nurses' station apart looking for it. I found it in about two minutes, rolled under the edge of the computer cart. He was so relieved, he bought me a coffee the next day. A large one, too, from that cafe down the street that roasts their own beans. Their coffee is amazing, but the line is always out the door. I don't know how people have the patience to stand in line for twenty minutes just for a cup of coffee. Especially in the winter when the wind is whipping down the avenue. It cuts right through your coat. But anyway... yeah, work is tough right now. I don't even go to chess nights with my buddies anymore, or take my camera out for walks. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game or even act normal around them. I feel like I'd just mess it up or say the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your job tasks, or has this feeling of 'cotton-brain' and the mistakes you're making caused you to stop working or require a leave of absence?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still working. I haven't taken a leave or anything, but... yeah, like I said, I'm making those dumb mistakes. I'll just completely blank on things, and the nurses have to tell me the same thing two or three times. It's just... I'm not myself. I've even stopped doing my normal stuff outside of work. Like, I used to go to chess nights with the guys, or take my camera out and just walk around the city, taking pictures. But I don't do any of that anymore. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game, or even act normal around them, you know? Speaking of chess, my friend Gary, he's got this beautiful wooden chess set. Hand-carved pieces from Poland or somewhere. The knights actually look like real horses, not just those blocky shapes you usually see. Gary's a bit of a fanatic, honestly. He watches all these grandmaster games on YouTube. He tried to teach me the Sicilian Defense once, but there are just too many variations. It’s like trying to memorize a phone book. Remember when they used to deliver those huge Yellow Pages to your door? They were so heavy, you could use them as a doorstop. My grandma used to keep one in her kitchen and she’d press flowers in it. She had this garden full of hydrangeas... but yeah, I just... I haven't been going to chess or doing much of anything. I just feel like I can't keep up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, I'll forget where I put the clean linens or I'll need the nurses to repeat simple requests, like two or three times. It's embarrassing. I just can't seem to focus. It's like my brain is full of cotton. Which is weird, because I used to be so sharp. I was the guy everyone came to when they needed something found quickly. I remember this one time, Dr. Henderson lost his favorite pen—this fancy silver one his wife gave him for their anniversary. He was tearing the nurses' station apart looking for it. I found it in about two minutes, rolled under the edge of the computer cart. He was so relieved, he bought me a coffee the next day. A large one, too, from that cafe down the street that roasts their own beans. Their coffee is amazing, but the line is always out the door. I don't know how people have the patience to stand in line for twenty minutes just for a cup of coffee. Especially in the winter when the wind is whipping down the avenue. It cuts right through your coat. But anyway... yeah, work is tough right now. I don't even go to chess nights with my buddies anymore, or take my camera out for walks. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game or even act normal around them. I feel like I'd just mess it up or say the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your job tasks, or has this feeling of 'cotton-brain' and the mistakes you're making caused you to stop working or require a leave of absence?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still working. I haven't taken a leave or anything, but... yeah, like I said, I'm making those dumb mistakes. I'll just completely blank on things, and the nurses have to tell me the same thing two or three times. It's just... I'm not myself. I've even stopped doing my normal stuff outside of work. Like, I used to go to chess nights with the guys, or take my camera out and just walk around the city, taking pictures. But I don't do any of that anymore. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game, or even act normal around them, you know? Speaking of chess, my friend Gary, he's got this beautiful wooden chess set. Hand-carved pieces from Poland or somewhere. The knights actually look like real horses, not just those blocky shapes you usually see. Gary's a bit of a fanatic, honestly. He watches all these grandmaster games on YouTube. He tried to teach me the Sicilian Defense once, but there are just too many variations. It’s like trying to memorize a phone book. Remember when they used to deliver those huge Yellow Pages to your door? They were so heavy, you could use them as a doorstop. My grandma used to keep one in her kitchen and she’d press flowers in it. She had this garden full of hydrangeas... but yeah, I just... I haven't been going to chess or doing much of anything. I just feel like I can't keep up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your inability to focus and the mistakes you are making led to any formal disciplinary action or warnings from your supervisor at the hospital?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, I haven't... I haven't been written up or anything like that. No formal warnings. Brenda, my supervisor, she's been pretty patient, I guess. The fluorescent lights flicker constantly, and there's this one pipe near the old laundry chute that makes a hissing sound every time you walk past it. I told Gary about it—he's the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—and he said it's just steam pressure, but honestly, it sounds like a snake. A really big, angry snake. And the floors down there are always slightly damp, which makes the wheels on the transport chairs squeak. It drives me crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on one of the chairs once, but my supervisor caught me and said it wasn't protocol. Protocol! Like we have a protocol for squeaky wheels in a damp, creepy basement. But yeah, no, I've never felt this down before. This is all new.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the changes in your appetite and feeling down, have you noticed that you've lost interest or pleasure in doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. I used to love going for runs in Van Cortlandt Park, you know? Taking my camera out, getting some good shots of the trees, maybe meeting up with a few buddies to play chess. But now... I just tell myself there's no point. I end up just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or staring at my budget spreadsheet. And speaking of the budget, the price of groceries has just gotten insane. I was at the store the other day just to get coffee, right? Because like I said, that's all I'm really having. And a regular bag of the store-brand coffee was almost nine dollars. Nine dollars! I remember when you could get a premium roast for that much. And don't even get me started on eggs. I don't eat them right now, but I saw the price tag and nearly had a heart attack. The cashier—I think her name tag said Brenda—she was telling me that people have been complaining all week. She looked exhausted, honestly. Being on your feet all day dealing with angry customers... it's a lot like being at the hospital, really. People take out their frustrations on the person right in front of them, even if it's not their fault. Like this one patient we had in room 412 last month... he threw his remote at the TV because the sports channel was blacked out. But anyway... yeah, I just don't have the energy or care to do the things I used to like. Just sit at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or do you feel so restless that you find it hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.\n\nDoctor: With all of this heaviness and the feeling that everything is pointless, have you found yourself having any thoughts that life is not worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't... I haven't made any kind of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But yeah, sometimes I do catch myself just wishing I wouldn't wake up. Like, it would just be so much easier if I could disappear, you know? Just poof, gone. No more dragging myself out of bed, no more dealing with the squeaky transport chairs. It reminds me of this magic show my uncle took me to when I was a kid. This guy, he called himself The Great Zamboni or something, he had this huge cabinet on stage. He put his assistant in there, closed the doors, spun it around, and when he opened it—she was gone. Poof. I always wondered how they did that trick. I mean, I know there's a trap door or mirrors involved. My cousin Eddie tried to explain it to me once. Eddie was always trying to figure out how things worked. He took apart his dad's lawnmower when he was twelve and couldn't put it back together. His dad was furious. They had to pay a guy fifty bucks to come fix it, and fifty bucks was a lot of money back then. Eddie ended up becoming an engineer, though, so I guess taking the lawnmower apart paid off in the end. He lives out in Seattle now. Rains a lot out there. I don't think I could handle all that rain. But yeah, I don't want to kill myself, I just... I just think about how nice it would be to just not be here anymore. Just disappear like that assistant in the cabinet.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your duties at work, or has your condition made it impossible for you to carry out your job responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's getting harder, honestly. I'm still showing up, but... I've started making these really avoidable mistakes. Like, I'll forget where I put the clean linens or I'll need the nurses to repeat simple requests, like two or three times. It's embarrassing. I just can't seem to focus. It's like my brain is full of cotton. Which is weird, because I used to be so sharp. I was the guy everyone came to when they needed something found quickly. I remember this one time, Dr. Henderson lost his favorite pen—this fancy silver one his wife gave him for their anniversary. He was tearing the nurses' station apart looking for it. I found it in about two minutes, rolled under the edge of the computer cart. He was so relieved, he bought me a coffee the next day. A large one, too, from that cafe down the street that roasts their own beans. Their coffee is amazing, but the line is always out the door. I don't know how people have the patience to stand in line for twenty minutes just for a cup of coffee. Especially in the winter when the wind is whipping down the avenue. It cuts right through your coat. But anyway... yeah, work is tough right now. I don't even go to chess nights with my buddies anymore, or take my camera out for walks. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game or even act normal around them. I feel like I'd just mess it up or say the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your job tasks, or has this feeling of 'cotton-brain' and the mistakes you're making caused you to stop working or require a leave of absence?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still working. I haven't taken a leave or anything, but... yeah, like I said, I'm making those dumb mistakes. I'll just completely blank on things, and the nurses have to tell me the same thing two or three times. It's just... I'm not myself. I've even stopped doing my normal stuff outside of work. Like, I used to go to chess nights with the guys, or take my camera out and just walk around the city, taking pictures. But I don't do any of that anymore. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game, or even act normal around them, you know? Speaking of chess, my friend Gary, he's got this beautiful wooden chess set. Hand-carved pieces from Poland or somewhere. The knights actually look like real horses, not just those blocky shapes you usually see. Gary's a bit of a fanatic, honestly. He watches all these grandmaster games on YouTube. He tried to teach me the Sicilian Defense once, but there are just too many variations. It’s like trying to memorize a phone book. Remember when they used to deliver those huge Yellow Pages to your door? They were so heavy, you could use them as a doorstop. My grandma used to keep one in her kitchen and she’d press flowers in it. She had this garden full of hydrangeas... but yeah, I just... I haven't been going to chess or doing much of anything. I just feel like I can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Has your inability to focus and the mistakes you are making led to any formal disciplinary action or warnings from your supervisor at the hospital?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't... I haven't been written up or anything like that. No formal warnings. Brenda, my supervisor, she's been pretty patient, I guess. She just kind of sighs and helps me fix whatever I messed up. She wears these reading glasses on a little beaded chain around her neck, so she's always pulling them up and dropping them back down when she looks at the clipboard. \n\nIt actually reminds me of my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had a chain for her glasses too, but hers was made of fake pearls. She loved pearls. She even had a pearl steering wheel cover in her car. It was this huge boat of a Buick. I remember because she gave me a ride home once when I missed the bus. It was raining so hard that day, the streets were basically rivers, and that Buick just glided right through the puddles. My dad used to have a LeSabre, same kind of floaty suspension. You'd hit a bump and the car would just bounce up and down for a mile. He loved that car. He'd spend every Sunday waxing it in the driveway... but, um... no, Brenda hasn't called me into the office or given me any disciplinary papers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, definitely not to the same standard. It's... it's definitely impaired. Like I was saying, I'm making those avoidable mistakes, and I just can't seem to hold onto a thought. The nurses have to repeat simple requests two or three times before it clicks. It's like I'm moving through molasses. And it's not just work, it's everything. I've completely stopped going to chess nights, and I haven't taken my camera out for a walk in weeks. I just don't trust myself to keep up with the game or act normal around the guys. 

My camera is a Nikon, actually. I saved up for six months to buy it. It’s got this incredible zoom lens, you can see the craters on the moon if the night is clear enough. I used to love going down to the harbor at night to take long exposure shots of the boats. The water looks like glass when you do that. You have to use a tripod, though, otherwise the picture comes out all blurry. I bought a really sturdy carbon fiber one. It’s super light but it doesn't shake in the wind. The wind down by the harbor can get pretty intense, especially in November. It just whips right off the water. You have to wear layers. My mom used to knit these thick wool scarves... she made me this one that was dark green, forest green, I guess you'd call it. It was so warm, but it was incredibly itchy. I could only wear it over a turtleneck... but yeah, I just... I can't do my job the way I used to. I'm definitely not functioning like normal.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. Smith. Well, I guess what brings me in is that my eating has just gotten so off lately. Like, some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before I clock in for my shift. I work as a healthcare support worker, and honestly, the coffee in our breakroom has been terrible ever since administration switched suppliers last month. It’s this cheap dark roast that just tastes like burnt dirt. I don't know who makes these purchasing decisions, probably someone in corporate who never even steps foot on our floor... but, um, anyway, yeah, I'm barely eating. \n\nAnd I noticed my scrubs have been hanging really loose on me lately, even though I'm not trying to lose weight at all. Which is annoying because I just bought these new ones with the extra cargo pockets on the thigh—you really need those pockets for all the tape and extra supplies when you're doing patient transport—and now they just sag right off my waist. The drawstring is pulled as tight as it goes.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food or eating less because you are worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. Like I said, I'm definitely not trying to lose weight. I just... I don't know, I just don't have the appetite for it. I look at food and it just seems like too much effort to even chew it. Which is weird because I used to love stopping by this little diner near the hospital after a long shift. They make this incredible meatloaf—like, real homestyle meatloaf with the crispy edges and the thick brown gravy. The waitress there, Shirley, she always knows exactly how I like my potatoes, too. Mashed, but with the skins still mixed in. She's been working there for, gosh, must be fifteen years. I'm not worried about gaining weight. I just barely eat my coffee and a granola bar, and that's it. And then my scrubs are just falling off me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your appetite, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's... well, most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge. It's just this weight in my chest. And speaking of my badge, the clip on it broke last week, the retractable kind? I had to use a safety pin to attach it to my scrubs, which is just a hazard waiting to happen when you're helping patients out of bed. I put in a request for a new one with HR, but you know how they are. They said it takes three to five business days to process a replacement, but with the holiday weekend coming up, who knows when I'll actually get it. My friend Gary in maintenance said he waited two weeks for his. Two weeks! Just to get a piece of plastic with a string in it. But, um, yeah, I just feel really down and heavy most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way, or experienced a period of feeling this down and having such a low appetite, at any point in your life before now?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had bad days, sure, but nothing like this where I just... where I don't even want to eat and everything feels so heavy. I've always been a pretty steady guy. Like, even when they renovated the west wing of the hospital and we had to detour all the transport beds through the basement tunnels. Those tunnels are so creepy, by the way. The fluorescent lights flicker constantly, and there's this one pipe near the old laundry chute that makes a hissing sound every time you walk past it. I told Gary about it—he's the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—and he said it's just steam pressure, but honestly, it sounds like a snake. A really big, angry snake. And the floors down there are always slightly damp, which makes the wheels on the transport chairs squeak. It drives me crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on one of the chairs once, but my supervisor caught me and said it wasn't protocol. Protocol! Like we have a protocol for squeaky wheels in a damp, creepy basement. But yeah, no, I've never felt this down before. This is all new.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the changes in your appetite and feeling down, have you noticed that you've lost interest or pleasure in doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. I used to love going for runs in Van Cortlandt Park, you know? Taking my camera out, getting some good shots of the trees, maybe meeting up with a few buddies to play chess. But now... I just tell myself there's no point. I end up just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or staring at my budget spreadsheet. And speaking of the budget, the price of groceries has just gotten insane. I was at the store the other day just to get coffee, right? Because like I said, that's all I'm really having. And a regular bag of the store-brand coffee was almost nine dollars. Nine dollars! I remember when you could get a premium roast for that much. And don't even get me started on eggs. I don't eat them right now, but I saw the price tag and nearly had a heart attack. The cashier—I think her name tag said Brenda—she was telling me that people have been complaining all week. She looked exhausted, honestly. Being on your feet all day dealing with angry customers... it's a lot like being at the hospital, really. People take out their frustrations on the person right in front of them, even if it's not their fault. Like this one patient we had in room 412 last month... he threw his remote at the TV because the sports channel was blacked out. But anyway... yeah, I just don't have the energy or care to do the things I used to like. Just sit at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or do you feel so restless that you find it hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's... it's kind of both, I think. Like, at work, sometimes I can't stop moving. I'll just pace around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over, checking the gauze and the tape. And speaking of tape, the hospital just switched suppliers for the medical tape, and this new stuff is terrible. It doesn't stick to anything. I was trying to secure an IV line for a nurse the other day—Nurse Sarah, she's great, always brings donuts on Fridays, though I haven't been eating them lately—and the tape just kept peeling off the patient's skin. We had to use three pieces just to get it to hold. And the dispenser they gave us for the new tape is this cheap plastic thing that jams every time you try to tear a piece off. I spent like ten minutes just trying to fix the dispenser while Sarah was standing there waiting. It's so frustrating when they try to save a few pennies and end up making our jobs ten times harder. But then, on the flip side, sometimes I feel like I'm moving through molasses. Like, drawing up labels or just answering a patient when they call for help... it takes me twice as long as it should. My body just feels so heavy and slow. So yeah, it's like I'm either bouncing off the walls checking drawers or I can barely put one foot in front of the other.\n\nDoctor: With all of this heaviness and the feeling that everything is pointless, have you found yourself having any thoughts that life is not worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't... I haven't made any kind of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But yeah, sometimes I do catch myself just wishing I wouldn't wake up. Like, it would just be so much easier if I could disappear, you know? Just poof, gone. No more dragging myself out of bed, no more dealing with the squeaky transport chairs. It reminds me of this magic show my uncle took me to when I was a kid. This guy, he called himself The Great Zamboni or something, he had this huge cabinet on stage. He put his assistant in there, closed the doors, spun it around, and when he opened it—she was gone. Poof. I always wondered how they did that trick. I mean, I know there's a trap door or mirrors involved. My cousin Eddie tried to explain it to me once. Eddie was always trying to figure out how things worked. He took apart his dad's lawnmower when he was twelve and couldn't put it back together. His dad was furious. They had to pay a guy fifty bucks to come fix it, and fifty bucks was a lot of money back then. Eddie ended up becoming an engineer, though, so I guess taking the lawnmower apart paid off in the end. He lives out in Seattle now. Rains a lot out there. I don't think I could handle all that rain. But yeah, I don't want to kill myself, I just... I just think about how nice it would be to just not be here anymore. Just disappear like that assistant in the cabinet.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your duties at work, or has your condition made it impossible for you to carry out your job responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, it's getting harder, honestly. I'm still showing up, but... I've started making these really avoidable mistakes. Like, I'll forget where I put the clean linens or I'll need the nurses to repeat simple requests, like two or three times. It's embarrassing. I just can't seem to focus. It's like my brain is full of cotton. Which is weird, because I used to be so sharp. I was the guy everyone came to when they needed something found quickly. I remember this one time, Dr. Henderson lost his favorite pen—this fancy silver one his wife gave him for their anniversary. He was tearing the nurses' station apart looking for it. I found it in about two minutes, rolled under the edge of the computer cart. He was so relieved, he bought me a coffee the next day. A large one, too, from that cafe down the street that roasts their own beans. Their coffee is amazing, but the line is always out the door. I don't know how people have the patience to stand in line for twenty minutes just for a cup of coffee. Especially in the winter when the wind is whipping down the avenue. It cuts right through your coat. But anyway... yeah, work is tough right now. I don't even go to chess nights with my buddies anymore, or take my camera out for walks. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game or even act normal around them. I feel like I'd just mess it up or say the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to perform your job tasks, or has this feeling of 'cotton-brain' and the mistakes you're making caused you to stop working or require a leave of absence?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still working. I haven't taken a leave or anything, but... yeah, like I said, I'm making those dumb mistakes. I'll just completely blank on things, and the nurses have to tell me the same thing two or three times. It's just... I'm not myself. I've even stopped doing my normal stuff outside of work. Like, I used to go to chess nights with the guys, or take my camera out and just walk around the city, taking pictures. But I don't do any of that anymore. I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with the game, or even act normal around them, you know? Speaking of chess, my friend Gary, he's got this beautiful wooden chess set. Hand-carved pieces from Poland or somewhere. The knights actually look like real horses, not just those blocky shapes you usually see. Gary's a bit of a fanatic, honestly. He watches all these grandmaster games on YouTube. He tried to teach me the Sicilian Defense once, but there are just too many variations. It’s like trying to memorize a phone book. Remember when they used to deliver those huge Yellow Pages to your door? They were so heavy, you could use them as a doorstop. My grandma used to keep one in her kitchen and she’d press flowers in it. She had this garden full of hydrangeas... but yeah, I just... I haven't been going to chess or doing much of anything. I just feel like I can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Has your inability to focus and the mistakes you are making led to any formal disciplinary action or warnings from your supervisor at the hospital?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't... I haven't been written up or anything like that. No formal warnings. Brenda, my supervisor, she's been pretty patient, I guess. She just kind of sighs and helps me fix whatever I messed up. She wears these reading glasses on a little beaded chain around her neck, so she's always pulling them up and dropping them back down when she looks at the clipboard. \n\nIt actually reminds me of my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had a chain for her glasses too, but hers was made of fake pearls. She loved pearls. She even had a pearl steering wheel cover in her car. It was this huge boat of a Buick. I remember because she gave me a ride home once when I missed the bus. It was raining so hard that day, the streets were basically rivers, and that Buick just glided right through the puddles. My dad used to have a LeSabre, same kind of floaty suspension. You'd hit a bump and the car would just bounce up and down for a mile. He loved that car. He'd spend every Sunday waxing it in the driveway... but, um... no, Brenda hasn't called me into the office or given me any disciplinary papers.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to carry out your work responsibilities to the same standard as you did before you started feeling this way, or would you say your ability to function at work is significantly impaired?\n\nPatient: Well, no, definitely not to the same standard. It's... it's definitely impaired. Like I was saying, I'm making those avoidable mistakes, and I just can't seem to hold onto a thought. The nurses have to repeat simple requests two or three times before it clicks. It's like I'm moving through molasses. And it's not just work, it's everything. I've completely stopped going to chess nights, and I haven't taken my camera out for a walk in weeks. I just don't trust myself to keep up with the game or act normal around the guys. \n\nMy camera is a Nikon, actually. I saved up for six months to buy it. My mom used to knit these thick wool scarves... she made me this one that was dark green, forest green, I guess you'd call it. It was so warm, but it was incredibly itchy. I could only wear it over a turtleneck... but yeah, I just... I can't do my job the way I used to. I'm definitely not functioning like normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
